Quantitative Disorder Effects in Low-Dimensional Spin Systems
Authors: Paul Dario, Matan Harel, Ron Peled
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
The Imry–Ma phenomenon, predicted in 1975 by Imry and Ma and rigorously established in 1989 by Aizenman and Wehr, states that first-order phase transitions of low-dimensional spin systems are ‘rounded’ by the addition of a quenched random field coupled to the quantity undergoing the transition. The phenomenon applies to a wide class of spin systems in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 2$$\end{document}d≤2 and to spin systems possessing a continuous symmetry in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4. This work provides quantitative estimates for the Imry–Ma phenomenon: in a cubic domain of side length L, we study the effect of the boundary conditions on the spatial and thermal average of the quantity coupled to the random field. We show that the boundary effect diminishes at least as fast as an inverse power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \log L$$\end{document}loglogL in general two-dimensional spin systems. For systems possessing a continuous symmetry, we show that the boundary effect diminishes at least as fast as an inverse power of L in two and three dimensions and at least as fast as an inverse power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \log L$$\end{document}loglogL in four dimensions. Finally, we establish a partial uniqueness results for translation-covariant Gibbs states, and prove that, for almost every realization of the random field, all such states must agree on the thermally-averaged value of the quantity coupled to the random field. Specific models of interest for the obtained results include the random-field q-state Potts model, the Edwards-Anderson spin glass model, and the random-field spin O(n) models.

Introduction

The large-scale properties of equilibrium statistical physics systems with quenched (frozen-in) disorder can differ significantly from those of the corresponding non-disordered systems [12, 58–60]. The present understanding of such phenomena is still lacking, in both the physical and mathematical literature, but some cases are better understood. This work focuses on the so-called Imry–Ma phenomenon, by which first-order phase transitions of low-dimensional spin systems are ‘rounded’ by the addition of a quenched random field to the quantity undergoing the transition. Imry and Ma [48] studied the Ising and spin O(n) models and predicted that, in low dimensions, the addition of a random independent magnetic field causes the systems to lose their characteristic low-temperature ordered states, even when the strength of the added field is arbitrarily weak. Specifically, they predicted this effect for the random-field Ising model at all temperatures (including zero temperature) in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 2$$\end{document}d≤2, and for the random-field spin O(n)-model, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, at all temperatures in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 4$$\end{document}d≤4. Their predictions were confirmed, and greatly extended, in the seminal work of Aizenman and Wehr [6, 7], who rigorously established the rounding phenomenon for a general class of spin systems in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 2$$\end{document}d≤2 and for spin systems with suitable continuous symmetry in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 4$$\end{document}d≤4.

Let us informally describe the Aizenman–Wehr result. Consider a spin system on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd, with a formal translation-invariant Hamiltonian H. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =(\eta _v)_{v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^d}$$\end{document}η=(ηv)v∈Zd be independent and identically distributed random variables (or random vectors). Construct a disordered spin system by modifying the Hamiltonian H to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda }(\sigma ):= H(\sigma ) - \lambda \sum _{v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^d} \eta _v\cdot f({\mathcal {T}}_{-v}(\sigma )) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hη,λ(σ):=H(σ)-λ∑v∈Zdηv·f(T-v(σ))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}T denotes the translation operator defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {T}}_v(\sigma )_u=\sigma _{u-v}$$\end{document}Tv(σ)u=σu-v, where f is the observable to which the random field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η is coupled, and where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ is the disorder strength. Aizenman and Wehr prove that, under suitable assumptions on H, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η and f, in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 2$$\end{document}d≤2, at all temperatures and positive disorder strengths, the limit1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{L \rightarrow \infty } \frac{1}{\left| \Lambda _L \right| } \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left\langle f ({\mathcal {T}}_v (\sigma )) \right\rangle _{\mu } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limL→∞1ΛL∑v∈ΛLf(Tv(σ))μexists with probability one (in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η), and takes the same value for all infinite-volume Gibbs measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ of the disordered system. The notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot \right\rangle _\mu $$\end{document}·μ denotes the thermal average over the Gibbs measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ; it should be noted that, while the set of Gibbs measures of the disordered system depends on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η, Aizenman and Wehr further prove that the common limit (1.1) does not depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. An analogous statement is proved to hold in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4 when the spin system and the added disorder satisfy suitable hypotheses of continuous symmetry. Additional information on the Aizenman–Wehr setup and result, and their comparison with those of the present work, is provided in Sect. 9.

The Aizenman–Wehr theorem does not provide quantitative estimates on the rate of convergence in (1.1) and it is the goal of this work to obtain such a quantified result. Specifically, the quantity that we study is1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2} \left| \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left( \left\langle f ({\mathcal {T}}_v (\sigma )) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f ({\mathcal {T}}_v (\sigma )) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right) \right| \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supτ1,τ21|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLf(Tv(σ))ΛL1-f(Tv(σ))ΛL2where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot \right\rangle _\Lambda ^\tau $$\end{document}·Λτ is the thermal average under the finite-volume Gibbs measure (of the disordered system) in the volume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Λ⊆Zd with boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L:= \left\{ -L, \ldots , L \right\} ^d\subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}ΛL:=-L,…,Ld⊆Zd and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Lambda _L \right| $$\end{document}ΛL is its cardinality, and where the supremum is taken over all choices of boundary conditions. We are thus measuring the largest discrepancy in the value of the spatially and thermally averaged observable, which may arise when changing the boundary conditions in finite volume. This quantity may be thought of as a particular kind of correlation decay rate. We emphasize that the boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _1,\tau _2$$\end{document}τ1,τ2 appearing in (1.2) are allowed to depend on L and on the specific realization of the disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η.

Our main results are that the following holds with high probability over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η: (1) For a general class of two-dimensional spin systems the quantity (1.2) decays at least as fast as an inverse power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \log L$$\end{document}loglogL, (2) For a general class of spin systems with continuous symmetry, the quantity (1.2) decays at least as fast as an inverse power of L in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2,3$$\end{document}d=2,3 and at least as fast as an inverse power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \log L$$\end{document}loglogL in dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=4$$\end{document}d=4. These results are shown to hold at all temperatures and positive disorder strengths.

Quantitative estimates of the type obtained here have recently been developed for the two-dimensional random-field Ising model [4, 5, 23, 34], where it was shown that correlations decay at an exponential rate (in the nearest-neighbor case) at all temperatures and positive disorder strength; see Sect. 4 for more details. However, to our knowledge, this is the only spin system for which quantitative estimates have previously been developed, and their derivation appears to crucially rely on monotonicity properties of the Ising model which are absent for general spin systems. Specific examples of interest for the results obtained here include the two-dimensional random-field q-state Potts (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge 3$$\end{document}q≥3) and Edwards-Anderson spin glass models, and the d-dimensional random-field spin O(n) models (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2) in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4.

We further point out that, while the assumptions placed by our results on the spin system, disorder distribution and noised observable are different, and in many ways more restrictive than those imposed by Aizenman–Wehr, an advantage of our methods is that they apply also to systems which are not invariant under translations (see also Sect. 9).

In spin systems satisfying suitable monotonicity properties, such as the random-field Ising model, the Aizenman–Wehr result allows to conclude that the disordered system possesses a unique Gibbs measure at all temperatures. Such a conclusion is false for general spin systems, but we provide a related conjecture pertaining to the possible general behavior (Sect. 2.3.2). The conjecture extends the well-known belief that the two-dimensional Edwards-Anderson spin glass model possesses a unique ground-state pair (see, e.g., [8, 55–57] and the references therein).

The rest of the paper is structured as follows: Sect. 2 presents the setup and results for general two-dimensional spin systems. The setup and results for spin systems with continuous symmetry are presented in Sect. 3. Section 4 is devoted to an overview of related results. An outline of our proofs is given in Sect. 5. Section 6 collects preliminary properties of the spin systems that we study. The proofs of the results pertaining to general two-dimensional spin systems are presented in Sect. 7 while the proofs pertaining to spin systems with continuous symmetry are presented in Sect. 8. Section 9 discusses additional points and highlights several open problems.

Two-Dimensional Disordered Spin Systems

In this section we describe the quantitative decay rates that are obtained for a general class of two-dimensional disordered spin systems. We first describe the spin systems to which the results apply (Sect. 2.1), then proceed to a list of specific examples which clarify and add interest to the general definitions (Sect. 2.2), and finally describe the results themselves (Sect. 2.3).

The general setup

We work on the standard integer lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd, in which we denote the standard orthonormal basis by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_1, \ldots , e_d$$\end{document}e1,…,ed; we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \sim v$$\end{document}u∼v if two vertices u, v are nearest neighbors. We let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Our general results (which do not rely on continuous symmetry) apply to disordered spin systems defined via (2.3) and (2.4) below and built as follows.

The base system: A non-disordered spin system is constructed from the following elements. State space and configuration space: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathcal {S}}, {\mathcal {A}}, \kappa )$$\end{document}(S,A,κ) be a probability space. The additional flexibility of the general definitions allows for inhomogeneities in the base system and further allows to vary the noised observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)$$\end{document}(fv) periodically along a sublattice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd (this may make sense, e.g., in antiferromagnetic systems, where the ordered state of the base system is not invariant to all translations), or even to choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)$$\end{document}(fv) arbitrarily.

The periodic measure must also satisfy the same consistency conditions described above. Below, whenever we take suprema over possible boundary conditions, we include the periodic measure.

Bounded boundary effect: We assume that there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_H \ge 0$$\end{document}CH≥0 such that for each finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Λ⊆Zd, 2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| H_{\Lambda } (\sigma ) - H_{\Lambda } (\sigma ')\right| \le C_H \left| \partial \Lambda \right| \quad \text {for }\, \sigma , \sigma ':{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathcal {S}}\, \text { satisfying }\, \sigma _\Lambda = \sigma '_\Lambda .\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ)-HΛ(σ′)≤CH∂Λforσ,σ′:Zd→SsatisfyingσΛ=σΛ′.

\usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda '\subseteq \Lambda $$\end{document}Λ′⊆Λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}η:Zd→R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda > 0$$\end{document}λ>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^d\setminus \Lambda }$$\end{document}τ∈SZd\Λ and any bounded measurable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g: {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^d} \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}g:Sd→R, 2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\langle \left\langle g(\sigma ) \right\rangle _{\Lambda '}^{\tau } \right\rangle _{\Lambda }^{\tau _0} = \left\langle g(\sigma ) \right\rangle _{\Lambda }^{\tau _0}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(σ)Λ′τΛ0=g(σ)Λ0, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} the restriction to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d\setminus \Lambda '$$\end{document}Zd\Λ′ of a random spin configuration distributed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\beta , \Lambda , \tau _0}^{\eta , \lambda }$$\end{document}μβ,Λ,τ0η,λ.

Examples

We now describe several classical examples of disordered systems which fit within the general class of systems discussed in the previous section. To the best of our knowledge, our general results are already new when specialized to these examples except for the example of the random-field ferromagnetic Ising model where stronger results are known (see Sect. 4).Random-field Ising model: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic Ising model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\Lambda }(\sigma ):= -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } h \sigma _v, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσuσv-∑v∈Λhσv, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}h∈R. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ):= \sigma _v$$\end{document}fv(σ):=σv so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=1$$\end{document}m=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0 and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda }_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left( \lambda \eta _{v} + h \right) \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσuσv-∑v∈Λληv+hσv corresponding to the addition of a quenched random field to the Ising model. One similarly forms the random-field antiferromagnetic Ising model by removing the minus sign in front of the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum \sigma _u \sigma _v$$\end{document}∑σuσv.Random-field q-state Potts model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge 3$$\end{document}q≥3 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{1, \ldots , q\}$$\end{document}S:={1,…,q} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic q-state Potts model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} 1_{\left\{ \sigma _u = \sigma _v \right\} } - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \sum _{k = 1}^q h_k 1_{\{ \sigma _v = k\}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v1σu=σv-∑v∈Λ∑k=1qhk1{σv=k}, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:= (h_1, \ldots , h_q) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}h:=(h1,…,hq)∈Rq. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ):= \big ( 1_{\{\sigma _v = 1\}}, \ldots , 1_{\{\sigma _v = q\}} \big )$$\end{document}fv(σ):=(1{σv=1},…,1{σv=q}) so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=q$$\end{document}m=q, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0 and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda , h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} 1_{\{ \sigma _u = \sigma _v \}} - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \sum _{k = 1}^q \left( \lambda \eta _{v, k} + h_k \right) 1_{\{ \sigma _v = k\}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v1{σu=σv}-∑v∈Λ∑k=1qληv,k+hk1{σv=k} where we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _v = \left( \eta _{v,1}, \ldots , \eta _{v,q} \right) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}ηv=ηv,1,…,ηv,q∈Rq and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = \left( h_1, \ldots , h_q \right) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}h=h1,…,hq∈Rq. The effect of the disorder is to add an energetic bonus or penalty to each spin state at each vertex according to quenched random vectors of length q which are assigned independently to the vertices.Edwards-Anderson spin glass: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (a uniform system) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=0. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ):=\left( \sigma _v \sigma _{v+e_i} \right) _{i \in \{ 1, \ldots , d \}}$$\end{document}fv(σ):=σvσv+eii∈{1,…,d} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=d$$\end{document}m=d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=1$$\end{document}R=1 and we have, in effect, a noise term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η assigned to every edge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd. The disordered Hamiltonian is (up to a term which does not depend on the spins in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda ,h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} (\lambda \eta _{u,v} + h) \sigma _u \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v(ληu,v+h)σuσv corresponding to adding a random energetic bonus/penalty to satisfied edges (edges with the same spin state assigned to both endpoints) independently between the different edges.Random-field spin O(n)-model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= {\mathbb {S}}^{n-1} \subseteq {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}S:=Sn-1⊆Rn equipped with the uniform measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the spin O(n) model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \cdot \sigma _v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσu·σv. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ) = \sigma _v\in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}fv(σ)=σv∈Rn so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = n$$\end{document}m=n and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta ,\lambda , h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \cdot \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left( \lambda \eta _{v} + h \right) \cdot \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσu·σv-∑v∈Λληv+h·σv corresponding to the addition of a quenched random field in a random direction, independently at each vertex, to the spin O(n)-model. We point out that while the results of the next section apply to the random-field spin O(n)-model, stronger results are obtained for it in Sect. 3 by relying on its continuous symmetry (at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=0$$\end{document}h=0).

Random-field Ising model: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic Ising model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\Lambda }(\sigma ):= -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } h \sigma _v, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσuσv-∑v∈Λhσv, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}h∈R. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ):= \sigma _v$$\end{document}fv(σ):=σv so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=1$$\end{document}m=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0 and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda }_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left( \lambda \eta _{v} + h \right) \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσuσv-∑v∈Λληv+hσv corresponding to the addition of a quenched random field to the Ising model. One similarly forms the random-field antiferromagnetic Ising model by removing the minus sign in front of the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum \sigma _u \sigma _v$$\end{document}∑σuσv.

Random-field Ising model: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic Ising model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\Lambda }(\sigma ):= -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } h \sigma _v, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσuσv-∑v∈Λhσv, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}h∈R. Random-field q-state Potts model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge 3$$\end{document}q≥3 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{1, \ldots , q\}$$\end{document}S:={1,…,q} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic q-state Potts model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} 1_{\left\{ \sigma _u = \sigma _v \right\} } - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \sum _{k = 1}^q h_k 1_{\{ \sigma _v = k\}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v1σu=σv-∑v∈Λ∑k=1qhk1{σv=k}, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:= (h_1, \ldots , h_q) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}h:=(h1,…,hq)∈Rq. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ):= \big ( 1_{\{\sigma _v = 1\}}, \ldots , 1_{\{\sigma _v = q\}} \big )$$\end{document}fv(σ):=(1{σv=1},…,1{σv=q}) so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=q$$\end{document}m=q, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0 and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda , h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} 1_{\{ \sigma _u = \sigma _v \}} - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \sum _{k = 1}^q \left( \lambda \eta _{v, k} + h_k \right) 1_{\{ \sigma _v = k\}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v1{σu=σv}-∑v∈Λ∑k=1qληv,k+hk1{σv=k} where we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _v = \left( \eta _{v,1}, \ldots , \eta _{v,q} \right) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}ηv=ηv,1,…,ηv,q∈Rq and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = \left( h_1, \ldots , h_q \right) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}h=h1,…,hq∈Rq. The effect of the disorder is to add an energetic bonus or penalty to each spin state at each vertex according to quenched random vectors of length q which are assigned independently to the vertices.

Random-field q-state Potts model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\ge 3$$\end{document}q≥3 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{1, \ldots , q\}$$\end{document}S:={1,…,q} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the ferromagnetic q-state Potts model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} 1_{\left\{ \sigma _u = \sigma _v \right\} } - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \sum _{k = 1}^q h_k 1_{\{ \sigma _v = k\}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v1σu=σv-∑v∈Λ∑k=1qhk1{σv=k}, for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:= (h_1, \ldots , h_q) \in {\mathbb {R}}^q$$\end{document}h:=(h1,…,hq)∈Rq. The effect of the disorder is to add an energetic bonus or penalty to each spin state at each vertex according to quenched random vectors of length q which are assigned independently to the vertices.

Edwards-Anderson spin glass: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (a uniform system) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=0. The disordered Hamiltonian is (up to a term which does not depend on the spins in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda ,h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} (\lambda \eta _{u,v} + h) \sigma _u \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v(ληu,v+h)σuσv corresponding to adding a random energetic bonus/penalty to satisfied edges (edges with the same spin state assigned to both endpoints) independently between the different edges.

Edwards-Anderson spin glass: the state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= \{-1, 1\}$$\end{document}S:={-1,1} equipped with the counting measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (a uniform system) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=0. The disordered Hamiltonian is (up to a term which does not depend on the spins in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda ,h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} (\lambda \eta _{u,v} + h) \sigma _u \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼v(ληu,v+h)σuσv corresponding to adding a random energetic bonus/penalty to satisfied edges (edges with the same spin state assigned to both endpoints) independently between the different edges.

Random-field spin O(n)-model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= {\mathbb {S}}^{n-1} \subseteq {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}S:=Sn-1⊆Rn equipped with the uniform measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the spin O(n) model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \cdot \sigma _v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσu·σv. We point out that while the results of the next section apply to the random-field spin O(n)-model, stronger results are obtained for it in Sect. 3 by relying on its continuous symmetry (at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=0$$\end{document}h=0).

Random-field spin O(n)-model: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2 be an integer. The state space is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}:= {\mathbb {S}}^{n-1} \subseteq {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}S:=Sn-1⊆Rn equipped with the uniform measure and the Hamiltonian of the base system (the spin O(n) model) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_\Lambda (\sigma ):=-\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \cdot \sigma _v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛ(σ):=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσu·σv. The noised observable is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma ) = \sigma _v\in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}fv(σ)=σv∈Rn so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R=0$$\end{document}R=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = n$$\end{document}m=n and the disordered Hamiltonian is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta ,\lambda , h}_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := -\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \{u, v\}\cap \Lambda \ne \varnothing \\ u\sim v \end{array}} \sigma _u \cdot \sigma _v - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left( \lambda \eta _{v} + h \right) \cdot \sigma _v \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=-∑{u,v}∩Λ≠∅u∼vσu·σv-∑v∈Λληv+h·σv corresponding to the addition of a quenched random field in a random direction, independently at each vertex, to the spin O(n)-model. We point out that while the results of the next section apply to the random-field spin O(n)-model, stronger results are obtained for it in Sect. 3 by relying on its continuous symmetry (at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=0$$\end{document}h=0).

Results

Main result

Throughout the section, we fix a disordered spin system of the type defined in Sect. 2.1, described by a state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathcal {S}}, {\mathcal {A}}, \kappa )$$\end{document}(S,A,κ), a family of Hamiltonians for the base system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H_\Lambda )_{\Lambda \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d}$$\end{document}(HΛ)Λ⊆Zd satisfying the bounded boundary effect assumption with constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_H$$\end{document}CH and a family of observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)$$\end{document}(fv) with a range of R, taking values in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^m$$\end{document}Rm. The disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\eta _v)$$\end{document}(ηv) is a collection of independent standard m-dimensional Gaussian vectors. The results of this section apply to two-dimensional spin systems so we further fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2.

In the first theorem, we present estimates on the effect of the boundary condition on the thermal expectation of the spatially-averaged noised observables in a finite box, in the presence of the disorder. For the second part of the theorem, recall that the notion of translation-invariant setup was defined in Sect. 2.1.

Here and later, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L:= \left\{ -L, \ldots , L \right\} ^d\subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}ΛL:=-L,…,Ld⊆Zd, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Lambda _L \right| $$\end{document}ΛL be its cardinality, and denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \cdot \right| $$\end{document}· the Euclidean norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^m$$\end{document}Rm.

Theorem 1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 be the inverse temperature and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 be the disorder strength. There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C,c>0$$\end{document}C,c>0 depending only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_H$$\end{document}CH, m and R such that, for each integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3,2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( \sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left( \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right) \right| > \frac{C}{\root 4 \of {\ln \ln L}} \right) \le \exp \left( - c L \right) \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Psupτ1,τ2∈SZ2\ΛL1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLfvσΛL1-fvσΛL2>ClnlnL4≤exp-cLand moreover, in a translation-invariant setup,2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( \sup _{\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \alpha - \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( {\mathcal {T}}_{-v} \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau } \right| > \frac{C}{\root 4 \of {\ln \ln L}} \right) \le \exp \left( - We mention that, for any fixed L, any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in \Lambda _L$$\end{document}v∈ΛL, and any boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau }$$\end{document}η↦fvσΛLτ is Lipschitz continuous, uniformly in v and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ. Thus, the quantities inside the probabilities in the previous theorems and the following ones are indeed measurable.

We remark that, while we only prove the result at positive temperature, the same argument yields the following zero-temperature version of the theorem: for a given side length L and a realization of the disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η, define an L-ground configuration as a configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \rightarrow {\mathcal {S}}$$\end{document}σ:Z2→S satisfying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{\Lambda _L}^{\eta ,\lambda }(\sigma ) = \inf _{\begin{array}{c} \sigma ':{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \rightarrow {\mathcal {S}}\\ \sigma '\equiv \sigma \, \textrm{in}\,{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L \end{array}} H_{\Lambda _L}^{\eta ,\lambda }(\sigma '), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛLη,λ(σ)=infσ′:Z2→Sσ′≡σinZ2\ΛLHΛLη,λ(σ′),and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_L^\eta $$\end{document}GLη be the set of L-ground configurations. Then we have2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( \sup _{\sigma _1, \sigma _2 \in G_L^\eta } \left| \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left( f_v \left( \sigma _1 \right) - f_v \left( \sigma _2 \right) \right) \right| > \frac{C}{\root 4 \of {\ln \ln L}} \right) \le \exp \left( - c L \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Psupσ1,σ2∈GLη1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLfvσ1-fvσ2>ClnlnL4≤exp-cLwhere, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_L^\eta $$\end{document}GLη is empty, then the supremum is assumed to take the value negative infinity. Additionally, if we assume that the state space is compact and both the base Hamiltonian and noised observables are continuous, we can show that, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η and all L, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_L^\eta $$\end{document}GLη of L-ground configurations is non-empty. It is straightforward that this is the case in all the example systems of Sect. 2.2.

In the translation-invariant setup, the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α is explicit for some of the models described in Sect. 2.2: for the q-state Potts model with external field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = 0$$\end{document}h=0, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \left( 1/q, \ldots , 1/q\right) $$\end{document}α=1/q,…,1/q. In the case of the Edwards-Anderson spin glass, a gauge symmetry (obtained by flipping the spins on the even sublattice and changing the sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η on all edges) implies that the density of satisfied edges is about 1/2, i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{L \rightarrow \infty } \frac{1}{\left| E \left( \Lambda _L\right) \right| } \sum _{\begin{array}{c} x, y \in \Lambda _L \\ x \sim y \end{array}} \left\langle 1_{\{\sigma _x = \sigma _y \}} \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _L} \underset{L \rightarrow \infty }{\longrightarrow }\ \frac{1}{2} \hspace{5mm}{\mathbb {P}}\text{-almost-surely } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limL→∞1EΛL∑x,y∈ΛLx∼y1{σx=σy}ΛLL⟶L→∞12P-almost-surelyfor any collection of random boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto \tau _L(\eta ) \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 {\setminus } \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}η↦τL(η)∈SZ2\ΛL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 1$$\end{document}L≥1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb}
