edges in the box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L$$\end{document}ΛL. In both these examples, our results are novel.

We finally remark that, while our result is stated and proved in dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 2$$\end{document}d=2, it is possible to adapt the argument to treat the case of the one-dimensional disordered spin systems. In this setting, the system is subcritical and power law decays can be obtained on the expectation of the supremum over pairs of boundary conditions of the difference of the thermally and spatially averaged noised observables. We record below (and without proof) the results one obtains by adapting the techniques developed in the proof of Theorem 1:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}\setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left( \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right) \right| \right] \le \frac{C}{L^{1/4}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Esupτ1,τ2∈SZ\ΛL1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLfvσΛL1-fvσΛL2≤CL1/4,and, in the translation-invariant setup,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \sup _{\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}\setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \alpha - \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( {\mathcal {T}}_{-v} \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau } \right| \right] \le \frac{C}{L^{1/6}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Esupτ∈SZ\ΛLα-1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLf0T-vσΛLτ≤CL1/6.

Uniqueness conjecture and additional results

Theorem 1 states that the spatial average over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L$$\end{document}ΛL of the thermal expectations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau }$$\end{document}fvσΛLτ does not depend strongly on the boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ. It is natural to ask for a more detailed result: to what extent can the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau }$$\end{document}fvσΛLτ at a given vertex v be affected by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ? Can the values at specific vertices v not too close to the boundary of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L$$\end{document}ΛL, or even at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \Lambda _L$$\end{document}v∈ΛL, be significantly altered by changing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ (keeping in mind that the overall spatial average does not depend strongly on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ in the sense of Theorem 1)? We conjecture that such a phenomenon cannot occur. We first state this as a conjecture, and then explain additional motivation as well as additional rigorous support (we remind that throughout the section we have fixed a disordered spin system of the type defined in Sect. 2.1 and we work in dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2)

Conjecture 2.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 be the inverse temperature and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 be the disorder strength. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}$$\end{document}P-almost-surely,2.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{L\rightarrow \infty }\sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left| \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right| = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limL→∞supτ1,τ2∈SZ2\ΛL1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLfvσΛL1-fvσΛL2=0and moreover, in a translation-invariant setup, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}$$\end{document}P-almost-surely,2.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{L\rightarrow \infty }\sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right| = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limL→∞supτ1,τ2∈SZ2\ΛLf0σΛL1-f0σΛL2=0.

We make several remarks about the conjecture:

In the translation-invariant setup, the pointwise statement (2.10) implies the averaged statement (2.9). This can be argued, for instance, by applying Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem to the functions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g_\ell (\eta ):=\sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _\ell }} \left| \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _\ell }^{\tau _1} -\left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _\ell }^{\tau _2} \right| . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gℓ(η):=supτ1,τ2∈SZ2\Λℓf0σΛℓ1-f0σΛℓ2.We also note that the pointwise statement (2.10) may be reformulated as a statement on the (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η-dependent) set of Gibbs measures of the disordered system. Indeed, let us assume that, in addition to having a translation-invariant setup, the state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}$$\end{document}S is Polish and compact, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {A}}$$\end{document}A the Borel sigma algebra, and the based Hamiltonian and noised observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v$$\end{document}fv are continuous (with respect to the product topology). These assumptions allow to extract a subsequential limiting Gibbs state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ out of a sequence of finite-volume Gibbs states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k$$\end{document}μk on increasing domains so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\mu _k}\rightarrow \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _\mu $$\end{document}f0σμk→f0σμ (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot \right\rangle _\mu $$\end{document}·μ is the expectation under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ). Then, the statement (2.10) is equivalent to the claim that, at any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta , \lambda >0$$\end{document}β,λ>0, it holds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}$$\end{document}P-almost surely that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _\mu $$\end{document}f0σμ takes the same value for all Gibbs measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ of the disordered system.

In monotonic systems such that, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1, \ldots , d \}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,d} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \in {\mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}L∈N, there exist boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\text {min}},\tau _{\text {max}}$$\end{document}τmin,τmax such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_{v,i} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _{\text {min}}}\le \left\langle f_{v,i} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau }\le \left\langle f_{v,i} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _{\text {max}}}$$\end{document}fv,iσΛLmin≤fv,iσΛLτ≤fv,iσΛLmax for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ and v (such as the plus and minus boundary conditions in the random-field ferromagnetic Ising model), the averaged statement (2.9) follows immediately from Theorem 1. If we additionally assume that we are in the translation-invariant setup described above, the pointwise bound (2.10) also follows.

Another statement which would be of interest to prove, and which is implied by the averaged statement (2.9), is the following: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}$$\end{document}P-almost-surely,2.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty }\lim _{L\rightarrow \infty }\sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \frac{1}{|\Lambda _\ell |} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _\ell } |\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} | = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limℓ→∞limL→∞supτ1,τ2∈SZ2\ΛL1|Λℓ|∑v∈Λℓ|fvσΛL1-fvσΛL2|=0.We also expect the following zero-temperature version of Conjecture 2.1 to hold: in both (2.9) and (2.10), replace the supremum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _1,\tau _2\in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^d{\setminus } \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}τ1,τ2∈SZd\ΛL by the supremum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _1,\sigma _2\in G_L^\eta $$\end{document}σ1,σ2∈GLη, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_L^\eta $$\end{document}GLη is the set of L-ground configurations and replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _i}$$\end{document}fvσΛLi by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma _i)$$\end{document}fv(σi). The zero-temperature pointwise statement would imply that, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}$$\end{document}P-almost-surely, all ground configurations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ (i.e., all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in \cap _L G_L^\eta $$\end{document}σ∈∩LGLη) agree on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma )$$\end{document}fv(σ) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}v∈Zd. In the specific case of the Edwards–Anderson spin glass model this is the same as the well-known belief that the two-dimensional model has a unique ground-state pair.

One way in which typical configurations of the system can fulfill Theorem 1 but avoid the uniqueness statement in (2.9) is if the configurations are differently ordered on the two bipartite classes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^2$$\end{document}Z2 (by the two bipartite classes, we mean the vertices with even sum of coordinates and the vertices with odd sum of coordinates). Such a situation is familiar from the (non-disordered) antiferromagnetic Ising model at low temperature, in which typical configurations have a chessboard-like pattern (and the boundary conditions can decide which of the two chessboard patterns will emerge). Our next result shows, as a special case, that such behavior cannot arise for the disordered systems considered herein, by showing that the boundary conditions cannot significantly influence the average value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle $$\end{document}fvσ on any deterministic set of positive density.

Theorem 2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 be the inverse temperature and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 be the disorder strength. There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C,c>0$$\end{document}C,c>0 depending only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_H$$\end{document}CH, m and R such that for each integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3 and for each weight function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:\Lambda _L\rightarrow [-1,1]^m$$\end{document}w:ΛL→[-1,1]m,2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} {\mathbb {P}}\left( \sup _{\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}} \left| \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|}\sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} w(v) \cdot \left( \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right) \right| \le \frac{C}{\root 8 \of {\ln \ln L}} \right) \nonumber \\{} & {} \qquad \ge 1 - \exp \left( - c L \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Psupτ1,τ2∈SZ2\ΛL1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLw(v)·fvσΛL1-fvσΛL2≤ClnlnL8≥1-exp-cL.

A second motivation for Theorem 2 comes from Parseval’s identity, which, in our setting, reads2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \frac{1}{|\Lambda _L|} \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} \left| \left\langle f_{v} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right| ^2\nonumber \\{} & {} \qquad = \sum _{ {\textbf{k}} \in \Lambda _L} \left| \frac{1}{\left| \Lambda _L \right| }\sum _{v \in \Lambda _L} e^{\frac{2\pi i\,{\textbf{k}} \cdot v}{2L+1}} \left( \left\langle f_{v} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1} - \left\langle f_{v} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _2} \right) \right| ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1|ΛL|∑v∈ΛLfvσΛL1-fvσΛL22=∑k∈ΛL1ΛL∑v∈ΛLe2πik·v2L+1fvσΛL1-fvσΛL22.Consequently, if one can upgrade the statement of Theorem 2 and obtain a rate of convergence faster than 1/L (instead of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\log \log L)^{-1/8}$$\end{document}(loglogL)-1/8 term), then it would imply that the right-hand side of (2.13) is small, which would then yield (2.9).

As a corollary of Theorem 2, we obtain a quantitative estimate in the spirit of the averaged statement (2.9). The obtained result is weaker than (2.9) as it involves the expected value (in the random field) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau (\eta )}$$\end{document}fvσΛLτ(η).

Corollary 2.2

If the weights w(v) were allowed to depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η, Theorem 2 would imply Conjecture 2.1. A partial result in this direction is formulated in Proposition 7.8, which strengthens Theorem 2 to allow w(v) to have a restricted dependence on the disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η.

As a second consequence of Theorem 2, we obtain a partial uniqueness result related to Conjecture 2.1. As mentioned above, in a translation-invariant setup, when the state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}$$\end{document}S is Polish and compact, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {A}}$$\end{document}A is the corresponding Borel sigma algebra, and when the Hamiltonian and noised observables are continuous, Conjecture 2.1 is equivalent to the claim that the expectation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}}(\sigma )\right\rangle _{\mu }$$\end{document}f0(σ)μ takes the same value for every infinite volume Gibbs measures and for almost every realization of the disorder. In Theorem 3 below, we establish this result (in the setup of general spin systems introduced in Sect. 2.1) in the specific case of translation-covariant Gibbs states defined in the following paragraph.

Let us fix a disorder strength \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 and an inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0. This definitions generalizes the notion of translation-invariant Gibbs states to the setup of disordered systems.

Theorem 3

(Uniqueness for translation-covariant Gibbs states). In the translation-invariant setup, for any inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0, any disorder strength \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0, and any pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _1, \mu _2$$\end{document}μ1,μ2 of infinite-volume translation-covariant Gibbs measures, one has the identity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}}(\sigma ) \right\rangle _{\mu _1^\eta } = \left\langle f_{{\textbf{0}}}(\sigma ) \right\rangle _{\mu _2^\eta } \hspace{3mm} {\mathbb {P}}-\text{ almost-surely. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f0(σ)μ1η=f0(σ)μ2ηP-almost-surely.

We note that we can produce a translation-covariant Gibbs state via compactness arguments (see Appendix I of Aizenman–Wehr [7]). However, even in the zero temperature case, it is difficult to obtain good control on the support of this measure for fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. An interesting question is to construct an extremal translation-covariant Gibbs state. Partial progress has been made in this direction: Cotar–Jahnel–Kulske [25] have shown the existence and measurability of a translation-invariant, extremal decomposition of translation-covariant random Gibbs states.

An example where such a construction could have far-reaching consequences is the zero-temperature Edwards–Anderson spin glass model. If one could show that there exists a translation-covariant Gibbs state supported on pairs of configurations that differ by a global sign change, then Theorem 3 implies that all translation-covariant Gibbs states are supported on such ground-state pairs.

As before, versions of Theorem 2, Corollary 2.2 and Theorem 3 hold at zero temperature. In Theorem 2, the supremum over boundary conditions should be replaced by a supremum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _1,\sigma _2\in G_L^\eta $$\end{document}σ1,σ2∈GLη and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_{v} \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _i}$$\end{document}fvσΛLi should be replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v(\sigma _i)$$\end{document}fv(σi). Spin Systems with Continuous Symmetry

We remark that the setting is sufficiently flexible to include periodic boundary conditions (i.e., taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ to be the discrete torus), as well as free boundary conditions. These are referred to by the notations per and free.The following results show that, in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le d\le 3$$\end{document}1≤d≤3, the spatially and thermally averaged magnetization is close to zero when the external field h is close to zero, uniformly in the boundary condition. The results depend on L through power laws. The first statement applies to deterministic boundary conditions, and has a better exponent in the power law when compared with the second statement, which is uniform in the (possibly random) boundary conditions (see Theorem 6 for more details on the case that h is large).

In particular the observables have range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = 0$$\end{document}R=0.

Disordered Hamiltonian: it will turn out to be useful to incorporate a deterministic external magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn in the disordered Hamiltonian. Thus, given additionally a finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Λ⊆Zd, a fixed realization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}η:Zd→R and a disorder strength \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda > 0$$\end{document}λ>0, we define 3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{\eta , \lambda , h }_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) := H_{\Lambda } \left( \sigma \right) - \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left( \lambda \eta _{v} + h \right) \cdot \sigma _v. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HΛη,λ,hσ:=HΛσ-∑v∈Λληv+h·σv. Given an inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0 and a boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda }$$\end{document}τ∈S∂Λ, we define the finite-volume Gibbs measure 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\beta , \Lambda , \tau }^{\eta , \lambda , h} \left( d\sigma \right) := \frac{1}{Z_{\beta , \Lambda , \tau }^{\eta , \lambda , h}} \exp \left( - \beta H^{\eta , \lambda , h}_{\Lambda }(\sigma ) \right) \prod _{v \in \Lambda } \kappa (d \sigma _v)\prod _{v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^d\setminus \Lambda }\delta _{\tau _v} \left( d\sigma _v \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μβ,Λ,τη,λ,hdσ:=1Zβ,Λ,τη,λ,hexp-βHΛη,λ,h(σ)∏v∈Λκ(dσv)∏v∈Zd\Λδτvdσv, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{\beta , \Lambda , \tau }^{\eta , \lambda ,h}$$\end{document}Zβ,Λ,τη,λ,h is the normalization constant. We use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \vee b:= \max (a, b)$$\end{document}a∨b:=max(a,b) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \wedge b:= \min \left( a, b \right) $$\end{document}a∧b:=mina,b for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a, b \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}a,b∈R.

Theorem 4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \{1,2,3\}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 1$$\end{document}L≥1. The next result presents our quantitative estimates in dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=4$$\end{document}d=4.

Theorem 5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 4$$\end{document}d=4. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 be the inverse temperature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 be the disorder strength and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h|\le 1$$\end{document}|h|≤1 be the deterministic external field. There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C>0$$\end{document}C>0 depending only on n, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ such that for each integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3,3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \sup _{\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda _L}} \left| \frac{1}{\left| \Lambda _L \right| } \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L}\left\langle \sigma _v \right\rangle ^{\tau , h}_{\Lambda _L} \right| \right] \le \frac{C}{\sqrt{\ln \ln \left( |h|^{-1} \wedge L \right) }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Esupτ∈S∂ΛL1ΛL∑v∈ΛLσvLτ,h≤Clnln|h|-1∧L.

We again remark that, in the second part of Theorem 4 and Theorem 5, free and periodic boundary conditions are allowed as one of the options in the supremum.

We mention that versions of Theorem 4 and 5 hold also at zero temperature. To state the results, we introduce the following notations. Indeed, this result is a consequence of the following observation: since the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau , \lambda , h}_{\Lambda , T=0} (\eta )$$\end{document}η↦FEΛ,T=0τ,λ,h(η) is concave, it is differentiable almost everywhere, and, on the corresponding set of full measure, the L-ground configuration with boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ is uniquely characterised as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{1}{\lambda }$$\end{document}-1λ times the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η-gradient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau , \lambda , h}_{\Lambda , T=0}$$\end{document}FEΛ,T=0τ,λ,h.

The zero-temperature version of the results is then obtained by replacing the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto \left\langle \sigma _v \right\rangle ^{\tau , h}_{\Lambda _{2\,L}}$$\end{document}η↦σv2Lτ,h in (3.4) by the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto \sigma _v$$\end{document}η↦σv with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in G_{2\,L}^{\tau , \eta }$$\end{document}σ∈G2Lτ,η. In (3.5) and (3.6), the supremum over boundary conditions should be replaced by a supremum over all L-ground configurations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in \cup _{\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda _L}} G_L^{\tau , \eta }$$\end{document}σ∈∪τ∈S∂ΛLGLτ,η and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \sigma _v \right\rangle ^{\tau , h}_{\Lambda _{L}}$$\end{document}σvLτ,h should be replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _v$$\end{document}σv.

Lastly, we mention that a discussion of models with continuous symmetries of higher order appears in Sect. 9.

Background

This section presents a brief overview of related results.

The random-field Ising model: The question of the quantification of the Imry-Ma phenomenon was previously addressed for the two-dimensional random-field Ising model. Chatterjee [23] obtained an upper bound at the rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\ln \ln L)^{-1/2}$$\end{document}(lnlnL)-1/2 on the effect of boundary conditions on the magnetization at the center of a box of side length L. Aizenman and the third author [5] studied the same quantity for finite-range random-field Ising models and obtained an algebraic upper bound of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-\gamma }$$\end{document}L-γ. Exponential decay was established first at high temperature or strong disorder; results in this direction include the ones of Fröhlich and Imbrie [40], Berreti [11], Von Dreifus, Klein and Perez [64], and Camia, Jiang and Newman [21]. Exponential decay at all disorder strengths was established for the nearest-neighbor model by Ding and Xia [34] at zero temperature, and then extended to all temperatures by Ding and Xia [35] and by Aizenman, the second and third authors [4]. Other important aspects of the random-field Ising model which have been the subject of recent developments include the correlation length of the model, which has been successfully identified by Ding and Wirth [33], and the absence of replica symmetry breaking which was established by Chatterjee [22]. We further refer to the recent work of Chatterjee [24] which investigates the presence, or the absence, of various properties of the mean-field spin glass model in the random-field Ising model (such as replica symmetry breaking, non-self averaging, ultrametricity, and the existence of many pure states).

Bricmont and Kupiainen [17] introduced a hierarchical approximation to the random-field Ising model and proved that it exhibits spontaneous magnetization in three dimensions and no magnetization in two dimensions. Imbrie [47] proved that the three-dimensional ground state of the Ising model in a weak magnetic field exhibits long range order, and this was extended to the low-temperature regime by Bricmont and Kupiainen [16, 18] using a rigorous renormalization group argument. Recently, Ding and Zhuang [36] obtained a new and simple proof of the result of [16, 18], extended the result to the q-states Potts model, and obtained a lower bound for the correlation length of the two-dimensional random-field Ising model at low temperature matching the one of [33]. These results were further extended by Ding, Liu, Xia [32] who proved that the model exhibits long range ordering in three and higher dimensions for any inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > \beta _c$$\end{document}β>βc (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _c$$\end{document}βc is the critical inverse temperature without disorder) as long as the strength of the disorder is sufficiently weak, and by Affonso, Bissacot and Maia [1] to the long-range Ising model.

Recently in [15], Bowditch and Sun constructed a continuum version of the two-dimensional random-field Ising model by considering the scaling limit of the suitably renormalized discrete random-field Ising model, and proved that the law of the limiting magnetization field is singular with respect to the one of the continuum pure two-dimensional Ising model constructed and studied by Camia, Garban and Newman [19, 20].

Additional background on the random-field Ising model can be found in [12, Chapter 7] and [54].

Random-field induced order in the XY-model: Crawford [28–30] studied the XY-model in the presence of a random field pointing along the Y-axis, and proved that this may lead to the model exhibiting residual ordering along the X-axis. We additionally refer to the references therein for a review of the works in the physics literature investigating this phenomenon in different models of statistical physics.

Other models of statistical physics: The Imry–Ma phenomenon has also been studied in the context of random surfaces. This line of investigation was initiated by Bovier and Külske [13, 14] with later, qualitative and quantitative, contributions of Cotar, van Enter, Külske and Orlandi [26, 27, 51, 52, 63]. Additional quantitative results are obtained in [31] which studies “random-field random surfaces” of the form4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}(d \phi ):= \frac{1}{Z} \exp \left( - \sum _{x \sim y} V \left( \phi _x - \phi _y \right) + \lambda \sum _{x} \eta _{x} \phi _x \right) \prod _x d \phi _x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(dϕ):=1Zexp-∑x∼yVϕx-ϕy+λ∑xηxϕx∏xdϕx,where in one case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is a mapping defined on the lattice and valued in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}R, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V: {\mathbb {R}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}V:R→R is a uniformly convex potential, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \phi _x$$\end{document}dϕx denotes the Lebesgue measure and in another case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is valued in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}Z, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(x)=x^2$$\end{document}V(x)=x2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \phi _x$$\end{document}dϕx denotes the counting measure. We refer to [31] for a more detailed review of this line of investigation.

A version of the rounding effect was also studied in models of directed polymers by Giacomin and Toninelli [43, 44]. They established that, while the non-disordered model may exhibit a first- (or higher-) order phase transition, this transition is at least of second order in the presence of a random disorder. Nevertheless, the mechanism taking place is of different nature than the one underlying the Imry–Ma phenomenon [44].

Quantum systems: The arguments developed in [7] were extended by Aizenman, Greenblatt and Lebowitz [3] to quantum lattice systems. They established that, at all temperatures, the first-order phase transition of these systems is rounded by the addition of a random field in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 2$$\end{document}d≤2, and in every dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \le 4$$\end{document}d≤4 for systems with continuous symmetry.

Strategy of the Proof

In this section, we outline the proofs of Theorem 1, Theorem 4 and Theorem 5, which contain the main ideas developed in the article. To simplify the presentation of the arguments, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = 1$$\end{document}m=1 in the definition of the noised observable and of the random disorder, that the strength \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ of the random field is equal to 1, and that the maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)$$\end{document}(fv) have range 0 (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_v$$\end{document}fv depends only on the value of the spin \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _v$$\end{document}σv).

For each realization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _L^\perp $$\end{document}ηL⊥, the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\eta }_L \mapsto -{{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau }_{\Lambda _L}({\hat{\eta }}_L, \eta ^\perp _L)$$\end{document}η^L↦-FELτ(η^L,ηL⊥) is convex, differentiable and 1-Lipschitz. Moreover, for any pair of boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^d{\setminus } \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}τ1,τ2∈SZd\ΛL, the finite-volume free energy satisfies the relation 5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau _1}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) - {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau _2}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) \right| \le \frac{C}{L}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FEL1(η)-FEL2(η)≤CL.

For each realization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _L^\perp $$\end{document}ηL⊥, the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\eta }_L \mapsto -{{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau }_{\Lambda _L}({\hat{\eta }}_L, \eta ^\perp _L)$$\end{document}η^L↦-FELτ(η^L,ηL⊥) is convex, differentiable and 1-Lipschitz. Moreover, for any pair of boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _1, \tau _2 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^d{\setminus } \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}τ1,τ2∈SZd\ΛL, the finite-volume free energy satisfies the relation 5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau _1}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) - {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau _2}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) \right| \le \frac{C}{L}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FEL1(η)-FEL2(η)≤CL.

The averaged external field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\eta }}_L$$\end{document}η^L is a Gaussian random variable whose variance is equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Lambda _L \right| ^{-1}$$\end{document}ΛL-1. The averaged external field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\eta }}_L$$\end{document}η^L is a Gaussian random variable whose variance is equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Lambda _L \right| ^{-1}$$\end{document}ΛL-1. In two dimensions, its fluctuations are of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{-1}$$\end{document}L-1, which is the same order of magnitude as the right-hand side of (5.2).

The Lebesgue measure of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}\setminus A_g$$\end{document}R\Ag is finite and satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Leb}\,}}\left( {\mathbb {R}}{\setminus } A_g \right) \le C/\delta ^2$$\end{document}LebR\Ag≤C/δ2.

The Lebesgue measure of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}\setminus A_g$$\end{document}R\Ag is finite and satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Leb}\,}}\left( {\mathbb {R}}{\setminus } A_g \right) \le C/\delta ^2$$\end{document}LebR\Ag≤C/δ2.

For each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in A_g$$\end{document}x∈Ag, and each differentiable, 1-Lipschitz and convex function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1: {\mathbb {R}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}g1:R→R satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup _{t \in {\mathbb {R}}}\left| g(t) - g_1(t) \right| \le 1$$\end{document}supt∈Rg(t)-g1(t)≤1, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| g'(x) - g_1'(x) \right| \le \delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g′(x)-g1′(x)≤δ.

For each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in A_g$$\end{document}x∈Ag, and each differentiable, 1-Lipschitz and convex function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1: {\mathbb {R}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}g1:R→R satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup _{t \in {\mathbb {R}}}\left| g(t) - g_1(t) \right| \le 1$$\end{document}supt∈Rg(t)-g1(t)≤1, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| g'(x) - g_1'(x) \right| \le \delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g′(x)-g1′(x)≤δ.

Applying this property to the free energies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs}
