Remark 7.6

Before giving the proof, we mention that, under the additional assumption that the Hamiltonians \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H_\Lambda )$$\end{document}(HΛ) satisfy the upper bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| H_\Lambda \right| \le C \left| \Lambda \right| $$\end{document}HΛ≤CΛ (as is the case for the models presented in Sect. 2.2), the free energy is a locally Lipschitz continuous function of the inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β, and thus, a small perturbation of the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β only slightly modifies the free energy. Consequently, the argument given below (which relies on the notion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ-stability introduced in Sect. 7.1) can be extended so that the result of Lemma 7.5 holds uniformly over the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β when this quantity belongs to a small open set.

Proof

The argument relies on Proposition 7.3 and Corollary 7.4. Using the upper bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) \le 2$$\end{document}FlucΛL(η)≤2, we may assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \le 2$$\end{document}δ≤2 without loss of generality. First, by the inequalities (6.5), we see that, to prove the inequality (7.9), it is sufficient to show, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{ 1, \ldots , m \}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,m} and any fixed boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _0 \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 {\setminus } \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}τ0∈SZ2\ΛL,7.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( {{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}_{\Lambda _L,i}^{\tau _0}(\eta ) < \delta + \frac{C}{L} \right) > \exp \left( - \frac{C}{\delta ^4} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PFlucΛL,i0(η)<δ+CL>exp-Cδ4.We now fix an integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1, \ldots ,m \}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,m} and prove (7.11). The estimate (6.8) can be rewritten as follows: for any boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{{\mathbb {Z}}^2 \setminus \Lambda _L}$$\end{document}τ∈SZ2\ΛL, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, i} \mapsto -{{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}^{\tau }_{\Lambda _L} \left( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, i}, \left( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, j} \right) _{j \ne i}, \eta ^{\perp }_{w,\Lambda _L} \right) $$\end{document}η^w,ΛL,i↦-FELτη^w,ΛL,i,η^w,ΛL,jj≠i,ηw,ΛL⊥ belongs to the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\lambda , r}(g).$$\end{document}Nλ,r(g).

The previous observation combined with the identity (6.7) yields the inclusion of sets\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ {\hat{\eta }}_{\Lambda _L, w,i} \in {\mathbb {R}}\,: \, \overline{{{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}}_{\Lambda _L,i}^{\tau _0}( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, i}, \left( \hat{\eta }_{w,\Lambda _L, j} \right) _{j \ne i}, \eta ^{\perp }_{w, \Lambda _L} ) > \delta \right\} \subseteq \textrm{Stab}(\lambda , \lambda \delta , r, g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η^ΛL,w,i∈R:Fluc¯ΛL,i0(η^w,ΛL,i,η^w,ΛL,jj≠i,ηw,ΛL⊥)>δ⊆Stab(λ,λδ,r,g).Applying Proposition 7.3 and the formula for the parameter r stated in (7.18), we deduce7.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{{\,\textrm{Leb}\,}}\left( \left\{ {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, i} \in {\mathbb {R}}\, : \, {\overline{{{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}}}_{\Lambda _L,i}^{\tau _0}( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, i}, \left( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L, j} \right) _{j \ne i} , \eta ^{\perp }_{w,\Lambda _L} ) \ge \delta \right\} \right) \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad&\nonumber \\&\le \frac{C \left| \partial \Lambda _L \right| }{ \delta ^2 \left| \Lambda _L \right| } + \frac{C}{ \delta ^2 \left| \Lambda _L\right| } \sum _{v \in \Lambda _{L} \setminus \Lambda _{\left( L - Using that the random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L,i}}$$\end{document}η^w,ΛL,i is Gaussian of variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Lambda _{\left( L - R\right) } \right| ^{-1} \ge c L^{-2}$$\end{document}ΛL-R-1≥cL-2, that the random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L,i}}$$\end{document}η^w,ΛL,i, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\hat{\eta }}_{w,\Lambda _L,j} \right) _{j \ne i}$$\end{document}η^w,ΛL,jj≠i and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{\perp }_{w,\Lambda _L}$$\end{document}ηw,ΛL⊥ are independent, and Corollary 7.4, we obtain that the inequality (7.19) implies the estimate (7.16).

The proof of the inequality (7.10) only requires a notational modification of the previous argument, we thus omit the details. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Mandelbrot percolation argument

In this section, we combine the result obtained in Lemma 7.5 with a Mandelbrot percolation argument to obtain the quantitative estimate (5.5). The result is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.7

Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 2$$\end{document}d=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda > 0$$\end{document}λ>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3. There exist two positive constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in (0,1)$$\end{document}c∈(0,1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C\in (1, \infty )$$\end{document}C∈(1,∞) such that7.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( {{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}_{\Lambda _L}(\eta ) < \frac{C}{\root 4 \of {\ln \ln L}} \right) \ge 1 - \exp \left( - c \sqrt{\ln L} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PFlucΛL(η)<ClnlnL4≥1-exp-clnL.

Upgrading the stochastic integrability

This section is the final step of the proof of Theorem 1 (in the general case). We use a concentration argument combined with the domain subadditivity property applied to the quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Fluc}\,}}_\Lambda $$\end{document}FlucΛ to upgrade the stochastic integrability obtained in Lemma 7.7.

The translation-invariant setup

In this section, we prove the estimate (2.7) pertaining to the translation-invariant setup and thus complete the proof of Theorem 1.

Proof of (2.7) of Theorem 1

We assume in this proof that the model is translation invariant and satisfies the corresponding additional assumptions stated in Sect. 2.1. We conclude this section by recording a stronger version of Theorem 2 where the weight are allowed to be (partially) random. The result is used in the proof of Theorem 3. Before stating the result, we introduce the following definition. We fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \in (0, 1)$$\end{document}ε∈(0,1) and, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in {\mathbb {Z}}^2$$\end{document}v∈Z2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{v}^\varepsilon , \eta _{v}^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}ηvε,ηv1-ε be m-dimensional Gaussian random vectors of mean zero and covariance matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon I_m$$\end{document}εIm and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\varepsilon ) I_m$$\end{document}(1-ε)Im, respectively. We assume that the Gaussian vectors are independent, and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta := \eta ^\varepsilon + \eta ^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}η:=ηε+η1-ε. In this setup, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η satisfies the assumptions required by the random disorder. We also let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}F1-ε be a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ-algebra such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}η1-ε is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}F1-ε-measurable and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}ηε is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}F1-ε-independent. Finally, we define the event7.44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {W}}_{1 - \varepsilon ,L,R} = \left\{ \sum _{v \in (x + \Lambda _{L_0}) \setminus (x + \Lambda _{(L_0-R)})} |\eta ^{1-\varepsilon }_v| \le 16 R L_0 \,: \forall x \in {\mathbb {Z}}^2, L_0 \in [\sqrt{L}, L] ~\text{ such } \text{ that }~ x + \Lambda _{L_0} \subseteq \Lambda _{L} \right\} .\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W1-ε,L,R=∑v∈(x+ΛL0)\(x+Λ(L0-R))|ηv1-ε|≤16RL0:∀x∈Z2,L0∈[L,L]suchthatx+ΛL0⊆ΛL.This events allows to control the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1$$\end{document}L1-norm of the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}η1-ε in the boundary layer of every cube \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ whose sidelength is at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{L}$$\end{document}L and is contained in the cube \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L$$\end{document}ΛL. Whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{1-\varepsilon } \in {\mathcal {W}}_{1 - \varepsilon ,L,R}$$\end{document}η1-ε∈W1-ε,L,R, the maximal contribution of this part of the field to the Hamiltonian restricted to a box of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document}L0 cannot exceed a constant multiple of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document}L0, mimicking the boundary dependence of the deterministic portion of the Hamiltonian.

We additionally note that, since the number of boxes contained in the box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L$$\end{document}ΛL with side length larger than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{L}$$\end{document}L grows as a power of L, a union bound combined with a large deviation estimate implies the following lower bound on the probability of the event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {W}}_{1 - \varepsilon ,L,R}$$\end{document}W1-ε,L,R7.45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {P}}\left( {\mathcal {W}}_{1 - \varepsilon ,L,R} \right) \ge 1 - C \exp (- c \sqrt{L}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PW1-ε,L,R≥1-Cexp(-cL).The next proposition extends the result of Theorem 2 by allowing the weight function to be partially random.

Proposition 7.8

Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 2$$\end{document}d=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda > 0$$\end{document}λ>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3. Proof of corollary 2.2

As a corollary of Theorem 2, we show that the absolute value of the expectation (with respect to the random field) of the thermal expectations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _0}$$\end{document}fvσΛL0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau _1}$$\end{document}fvσΛL1 is quantitatively small for any pair of random boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \mapsto \tau _0(\eta ), \tau _1(\eta )$$\end{document}η↦τ0(η),τ1(η).

Proof of Corollary 2.2

Proof of theorem 3

This section contains the proof of Theorem 3. The argument relies on an application of Proposition 7.8 combined with Levy’s zero–one law.

Proof of Theorem 3

Proofs for Spin Systems with Continuous Symmetry

In this section, we study the spin systems with continuous symmetry presented in Sect. 3 and prove Theorem 4 and Theorem 5. The section is organized as follows:In Sect. 8.1, we establish two variational lemmas on the set of bounded functions whose integral on any interval of the real line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}R is bounded.In Sect. 8.2, we implement a Mermin-Wagner type argument to prove an upper bound on the free energy (see Proposition 8.4).Sect. 8.3 is devoted to the proof of Theorem 4 in the subcritical dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 1,2,3$$\end{document}d=1,2,3. In Subsection 8.3.1, we combine the Mermin-Wagner upper bound obtained in Proposition 8.4 with Proposition 8.1 to prove the algebraic decay of the thermally and spatially averaged magnetization with fixed boundary condition stated in (3.4). In Subsection 8.3.1, we build upon the results of Subsection 8.3.1 and prove the inequality (3.5), thus completing the proof of Theorem 4.Sect. 8.4 is devoted to the proof of Theorem 5 following the outline of Sect. 5 and is divided into three subsections. Finally in Subsection 8.4.3, we upgrade the result of Lemma 8.7 to include a supremum over all the possible boundary conditions, and complete the proof of Theorem 5.

Variational lemmas

In this section, we state and prove two variational lemmas on the set of measurable bounded functions defined on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}R and whose integral on every interval is bounded in absolute value by 1, i.e.,8.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {G}}{} & {} := \Bigg \{ g: {\mathbb {R}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}\,: \, g \text{ is } \text{ measurable, } \text{ bounded } \text{ and, } \text{ for } \text{ any } \text{ real } \text{ interval } ~ I \subseteq {\mathbb {R}},\nonumber \\{} & {} \qquad \left| \int _I g(t) dt \right| \le 1 \Bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G:={g:R→R:gismeasurable,boundedand,foranyrealintervalI⊆R,∫Ig(t)dt≤1}.The first result we asserts that the Gaussian expectation of any map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in {\mathcal {G}}$$\end{document}g∈G is bounded by an explicit constant.

Proposition 8.1

One has the inequality8.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{g \in {\mathcal {G}}} \left| \int _{\mathbb {R}}g(t) e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt \right| \le 2 \int _0^\infty t e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supg∈G∫Rg(t)e-t22dt≤2∫0∞te-t22dt.

Proof

We fix a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in {\mathcal {G}}$$\end{document}g∈G, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(t):= \int _0^t g(s) ds$$\end{document}G(t):=∫0tg(s)ds. By the definition of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {G}}$$\end{document}G, the map G satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| G(t) \right| \le 1$$\end{document}G(t)≤1 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}t∈R, the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(0) = 0$$\end{document}G(0)=0, and is Lipschitz continuous. By performing an integration by parts, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hspace{25mm} \left| \int _{\mathbb {R}}g(t) e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt \right| = \left| \int _{\mathbb {R}}G(t) t e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt \right| \le \int _{\mathbb {R}}| t| e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt = 2 \int _0^\infty t e^{-\frac{t^2}{2}} \, dt. \hspace{25mm} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Rg(t)e-t22dt=∫RG(t)te-t22dt≤∫R|t|e-t22dt=2∫0∞te-t22dt.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The next proposition provides a lower bound on the Gaussian measure of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ g \le \delta \right\} $$\end{document}g≤δ, for any map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in {\mathcal {G}}$$\end{document}g∈G satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \ge -1$$\end{document}g≥-1, and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0.

Proposition 8.2

1}{1 + \delta } \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∀t≥0,Gt≥max0,2δt-11+δ.To prove (8.5), we use the assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \ge -1$$\end{document}g≥-1 and write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g&\ge \delta 1_{\{ g > \delta \}} - A mermin–wagner upper bound for the free energy

In this section, we obtain an upper bound on the free energy of a spin system equipped with a continuous symmetry by implementing a Mermin-Wagner type argument. We recall that the spin space is assumed to be the sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {S}}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Sn-1, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, as well as the notation for conditional expectations and probabilities introduced in Sect. 6.3 (as they will be used frequently in the proofs below). Before stating the result, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 8.3

(Free energy). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _0, \Lambda $$\end{document}Λ0,Λ be two boxes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \subseteq \Lambda _0.$$\end{document}Λ⊆Λ0. For any field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta : \Lambda _0 \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}η:Λ0→R, we denote by8.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\widetilde{\eta }}}_{\Lambda _0, \Lambda ,v}:= \left\{ \begin{aligned} \eta _v&~\text{ if }~ v \in \Lambda _0 \setminus \Lambda , \\ -\eta _v&~\text{ if }~ v \in \Lambda . \end{aligned} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η~Λ0,Λ,v:=ηvifv∈Λ0\Λ,-ηvifv∈Λ.We define the free energy, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta : \Lambda _0 \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}η:Λ0→R,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\widetilde{{{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}}}}_{\Lambda _0,\Lambda }^{\tau , h}(\eta ):= {{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}_{\Lambda _0}^{\tau , h} \left( {{\widetilde{\eta }}}_{\Lambda _0, \Lambda }\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FE~Λ0,Λτ,h(η):=FEΛ0τ,hη~Λ0,Λ.

The main result of this section is an upper bound on the difference of the free energies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}_{\Lambda _0}^{\tau ,h}$$\end{document}FEΛ0τ,h and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{{{\,\textrm{FE}\,}}}}_{\Lambda _0, \Lambda }^{\tau ,h}$$\end{document}FE~Λ0,Λτ,h conditionally on the values of the field in the box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ and outside the box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\Lambda $$\end{document}2Λ.

Proposition 8.4

(Mermin-Wagner upper bound for the energy). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \{1,2,3,4\}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3,4} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{ 1, \ldots , n\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,n}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 be the inverse temperature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 be the disorder strength and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn be the deterministic external field. Fix a box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _0 \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Λ0⊆Zd of side length L, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda _0}$$\end{document}τ∈S∂Λ0 be a boundary condition. Proof

Proof of theorem 4

In this section, we obtain an algebraic rate of convergence for the expectation of the spatially and thermally averaged magnetization in the subcritical dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \in \{ 1, 2, 3\}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3}. We prove the following more refined version of Theorem 4, which takes into account the dependence in the external magnetic field h. Theorem 4 is a direct consequence upon taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h| \le L^{-2}$$\end{document}|h|≤L-2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h| \le L^{-1}$$\end{document}|h|≤L-1.

Theorem 6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \in \{ 1,2,3 \}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 2$$\end{document}L≥2 be an integer, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn be a magnetic field satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h|\le 1$$\end{document}|h|≤1. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda _{2\,L}}$$\end{document}τ∈S∂Λ2L be a boundary condition (which may be the free or periodic boundary condition) and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell := |h|^{-\frac{1}{2}} \wedge L$$\end{document}ℓ:=|h|-12∧L. The proof of Theorem 6 is decomposed into two subsections: in Subsection 8.3.1, we establish the inequality (8.22), and in Subsection 8.3.2, we prove the upper bound (8.23).

Algebraic decay of the magnetization with fixed boundary condition

In this section, we combine Proposition 8.4 with Proposition 8.1 to obtain the algebraic decay of the magnetization with a fixed boundary condition stated in (8.22).

Algebraic Decay of the Magnetization Uniform Over the Boundary Conditions

In this section, we use the results established in Subsection 8.3.1 to obtain an algebraic decay for the magnetization which holds uniformly over the boundary condition.

Proof of theorem 5

The objective of this section is to prove Theorem 5 following the outline presented at the beginning of Sect. 8.

A lower bound on the conditional expectation of the spatially-averaged magnetization

In the first step of the proof, we show that, if the averaged value of the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η in a box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ is negative enough, then the thermally and spatially averaged magnetization of the continuous spin system with periodic boundary conditions in the box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ must be small. The argument relies on a combination of the variational lemma stated in Proposition 8.2 and of the Mermin-Wagner upper bound for the free energy (Proposition 8.4). Before stating the result, we introduce a notation for the quantile of the normal distribution which will be used in the statement and proof of Lemma 8.6.

Definition 8.5

(Quantile of the normal distribution). For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta > 0$$\end{document}δ>0, we define the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp \left( - \frac{1}{\delta ^2} \right) \right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tδ:=mint∈R:12π∫t∞e-s22ds≤1-exp-1δ2,Let us note that there exist two constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c, C \in (0, \infty )$$\end{document}c,C∈(0,∞) such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \in (0, 1/2]$$\end{document}δ∈(0,1/2],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -C \delta ^{-1} \le t_\delta \le -c \delta ^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Cδ-1≤tδ≤-cδ-1.

We then reformulate the inequality (8.46): using the formula (8.45) and a union bound, we obtain that there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C > 0$$\end{document}C>0 such that, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {P}}-$$\end{document}P-almost surely,8.47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} {\mathbb {P}}\left[ {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \frac{1}{\ell ^4} \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left\langle \sigma _{v,i} \right\rangle ^{\tau , h}_{\Lambda _0} ~ \Big \vert ~ {{\hat{\eta }}_{\Lambda ,i}}, \eta _{\left( \Lambda _0 \setminus 2\Lambda \right) , i} \right] \le C\delta ~ \Big \vert ~ \eta _{\left( \Lambda _0 \setminus 2\Lambda \right) , i} \right] \nonumber \\{} & {} + {\mathbb {P}}\left[ {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \frac{1}{\ell ^4} \sum _{v \in \Lambda } \left\langle \sigma _{v,i} \right\rangle ^{\tau , h}_{\Lambda _0} ~ \Big \vert ~ {{\hat{\eta }}_{\Lambda ,i}}, \eta _{\left( \Lambda _0 \setminus 2\Lambda \right) , i} \right] (-{\hat{\eta }}_{\Lambda ,i}, \eta _{\left( \Lambda _0 \setminus 2\Lambda \right) , i}) \le C\delta ~ \Big \vert ~ \eta _{\left( \Lambda _0 \setminus 2\Lambda \right) , i} \right] \nonumber \\{} & {} \ge \exp \left( - Mandelbrot percolation argument

In this section, we combine the result of Lemma 8.6 with a Mandelbrot percolation argument to obtain a quantitative rate of convergence on the expected value (with respect to the random field) of the spatially and thermally averaged magnetization with a fixed boundary condition.

Lemma 8.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 4$$\end{document}d=4. Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda >0$$\end{document}λ>0, a box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _0 \subseteq {\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Λ0⊆Zd, an integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\ge 3$$\end{document}L≥3 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{2\,L} \subseteq \Lambda _0$$\end{document}Λ2L⊆Λ0, and an external magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h| \le L^{-2}$$\end{document}|h|≤L-2. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathcal {S}}^{\partial \Lambda _{0}}$$\end{document}τ∈S∂Λ0 be a boundary condition (which may be the free and periodic boundary conditions). Proof of Theorem 5

In this section, we combine the result of Lemma 8.7 (applied with the periodic boundary condition) with an argument similar to the one developed in Subsection 8.3.2 to complete the proof of Theorem 5.

Proof of Theorem 5

Fix a side length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \ge 10$$\end{document}L≥10, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}h∈Rn such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| h \right| \le 1/10$$\end{document}h≤1/10, and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell := (L/2) \wedge |h|^{-1/2}$$\end{document}ℓ:=(L/2)∧|h|-1/2. Discussion and Open Problems

This work initiates the study of quantitative versions of the Aizenman–Wehr [6, 7] result on the Imry–Ma rounding phenomenon [48]. In this section we discuss some of the remaining problems:

Uniqueness conjecture: As discussed in Sect. 2, we believe that a stronger qualitative statement than the one provided by Aizenman–Wehr [6, 7] is valid. Namely, that in two dimensions the thermal averages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle f_v \left( \sigma \right) \right\rangle _{\Lambda _L}^{\tau }$$\end{document}fvσΛLτ cannot be significantly altered by changing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ in the sense of Conjecture 2.1. We again point out that Conjecture 2.1 would imply as a special case the well-known belief that the two-dimensional Edwards–Anderson spin glass model has a unique ground-state pair. It would be very interesting to make additional progress in this direction.

It is also possible that analogous uniqueness statements hold in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=3,4$$\end{document}d=3,4 for the spin systems with continuous symmetry discussed in Sect. 3 (the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2 is covered by Conjecture 2.1).

Quantitative decay rate and possible phase transitions: It is very natural to seek the optimal rates in our quantitative results. We first discuss the general two-dimensional disordered spin systems of Sect. 2. When the base Hamiltonian has finite range, exponential decay of correlations and uniqueness of the infinite-volume Gibbs measure follow in all dimensions in the high-temperature regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ll 1$$\end{document}β≪1), and for certain systems also in the strong disorder regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \gg 1)$$\end{document}λ≫1), from the disagreement percolation methods of van den Berg–Maes [62] and their adaptations by Gielis–Maes [45] (suitable versions of Dobrushin’s condition [37] should also be applicable). The main interest is thus in the low-temperature (or even zero temperature) regime. We then believe that the correct rate in two dimensions should be much faster than the inverse power of log-log rate obtained in Theorem 1. Without assuming translation invariance, the decay cannot hold at a faster than power-law rate (e.g., as the noised observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)$$\end{document}(fv) can be identically zero at all but one vertex), but it is possible that such a rate indeed holds in general. In the translation-invariant setup, it is even possible that exponential decay holds (noting that for a faster than power-law decay one needs to perform the spatial average in (2.7) over a smaller domain, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{L/2}$$\end{document}ΛL/2, to avoid boundary effects), as proved for the nearest-neighbor ferromagnetic random-field Ising model [4, 34] (though exponential decay is still open for the non-nearest-neighbor version for which only a power-law upper bound is known [5]).

We proceed to discuss the spin systems with continuous symmetry of Sect. 3. Exponential decay in the high-temperature regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ll 1$$\end{document}β≪1) again follows in all dimensions [45, 62]. This is also expected in the strong disorder regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \gg 1$$\end{document}λ≫1); see [38] for the zero-temperature case. The interest is thus in the low-temperature and weak disorder regime. One might expect exponential decay to hold in the sub-critical dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 2,3$$\end{document}d=2,3, and arguments have been given both in favor [2, 61] and, in the three dimensional case, against [39, 46] this possibility in the physics literature. Power-law decay would imply a transition of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless type [9, 10, 41, 49, 50] as the temperature or disorder strength varies and would thus be of great interest.

Higher-order continuous symmetries: The form of continuous-symmetry that the spin O(n) model enjoys is that its formal Hamiltonian\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(\sigma ):=\sum _{v\sim w} |\sigma _v - \sigma _w|^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(σ):=∑v∼w|σv-σw|2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {S}}^{n-1}$$\end{document}σ:Zd→Sn-1, satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(R\sigma ) = H(\sigma )$$\end{document}H(Rσ)=H(σ) for the operation R which rotates all spins in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ by the same angle. One can also envision spin systems enjoying higher-order symmetries in the sense that we now explain for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}n=2 case (analogous symmetries may exist for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n>2$$\end{document}n>2 but we have not explored this possibility). We embed the circle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {S}}^1$$\end{document}S1 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}C and write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _v = e^{i\theta _v}$$\end{document}σv=eiθv (where the angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ is defined modulo \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi $$\end{document}2π). For a polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P:{\mathbb {R}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}P:Rd→R and configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {S}}^1$$\end{document}σ:Zd→S1 define the ‘polynomial rotation’ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_P(\sigma )$$\end{document}RP(σ) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_P(\sigma )_v = e^{i (\theta _v + P(v))}$$\end{document}RP(σ)v=ei(θv+P(v)). A Hamiltonian H on configurations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :{\mathbb {Z}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {S}}^{1}$$\end{document}σ:Zd→S1 is then said to enjoy a continuous symmetry of order k if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(R_P(\sigma )) = H(\sigma )$$\end{document}H(RP(σ))=H(σ) for all polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P:{\mathbb {R}}^d\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}P:Rd→R of degree at most k. In particular, the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=0$$\end{document}k=0 corresponds to the usual notion of continuous symmetry. Higher-order symmetries reduce the surface tension of finite-range spin systems with smooth energy. Specifically, we believe that an analog of Proposition 8.4 holds for a spin system having a smooth, finite-range Hamiltonian enjoying a higher-order symmetry of order k with the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{d-2}$$\end{document}ℓd-2 replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{d-2k-2}$$\end{document}ℓd-2k-2. Specifically, spin systems with a finite-range smooth Hamiltonian enjoying a higher-order symmetry of order k will lose their low-temperature ordered phase upon introduction of a random field of arbitrarily weak intensity, of the form in our theorems, in all dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4(k+1)$$\end{document}d≤4(k+1). Moreover, the strategy used in this paper to obtain a quantitative decay rate can be followed to yield that (at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=0$$\end{document}h=0)9.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| {\mathbb {E}}\left[ \frac{1}{\left| \Lambda _L \right| } \sum _{v \in \Lambda _L } \left\langle \sigma _v \right\rangle ^{\tau , 0}_{\Lambda _{2L}} \right] \right| \le C L^{-2(k+1) + d/2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E1ΛL∑v∈ΛLσv2Lτ,0≤CL-2(k+1)+d/2in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<4(k+1)$$\end{document}d<4(k+1). Comparison with the results of Aizenman–Wehr: The seminal result of Aizenman–Wehr [6, 7] establishes rigorously the rounding of the first-order phase transitions of low-dimensional spin systems upon the addition of a quenched disorder. Our work presents a quantified version of the Aizenman–Wehr theorem, but applies in somewhat different generality than the original result. While we expect that the techniques developed in this work may be extended to a more general setup, closer to that of [6, 7], we have not pursued this direction. In this section, we elaborate on the various assumptions made:Translation-invariance of the systems: While the proof of Aizenman and Wehr requires to work in a translation-invariant setup, the techniques developed in this article apply to spin systems which do not satisfy this assumption.Distribution of the disorder: The result of Aizenman–Wehr applies to a wide class of disorder distributions while our result is presented only for the Gaussian case.Range of the interaction: The results of [6, 7] apply also to disordered systems with long-range interactions (in which case one-dimensional systems are also of interest) as long as these decay at a sufficiently fast rate. Our results for general two-dimensional disordered spin systems (Sect. 2) allow the base system to have arbitrary interactions as long as the bounded boundary effect condition (2.2) holds. However, we have opted to restrict to finite-range dependencies in the noised observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f_v)_{v \in {\mathbb {Z}}^d}$$\end{document}(fv)v∈Zd (Sect. 2.1).Uniformity of the results in the temperature and external field: The results of Aizenman–Wehr apply not only for a fixed value of the temperature and external magnetic field (the latter is incorporated into the models there) but also uniformly when these parameters are themselves allowed to depend on the disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. This uniformity shows that there cannot be deviations from the proven behavior at random critical points. In comparison, our results are stated only for a fixed value of the temperature and, in the general two-dimensional setup, without an external magnetic field (though one can be included in the base Hamiltonian). Still, a uniform version of our results may be obtained with minor modifications of the proof, as indicated in Remark 7.6.Systems with continuous symmetry: For systems with continuous symmetries, Aizenman and Wehr allow the spins to take values in a subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}Rn which is invariant under the action of a closed connected subgroup G of O(n), and require the base Hamiltonian to take the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{x,y} J_{x-y} \Psi _{x-y}(\sigma _x,\sigma _y)$$\end{document}∑x,yJx-yΨx-y(σx,σy) where each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _z$$\end{document}Ψz is required to be bounded by 1, to be invariant under the same subgroup, to satisfy certain differentiability properties and the coupling constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_z$$\end{document}Jz are required to satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{z:\left| z\right| \le L} J_z|z|^2\le C L^{(4-d)/2}$$\end{document}∑z:z≤LJz|z|2≤CL(4-d)/2. The noised observables are taken to be the spins themselves and the conclusion is then that in all dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4, a suitable spatially and thermally averaged magnetization value is invariant under all elements of G for all Gibbs measures of the model, almost surely. It is further mentioned that the technique should generalize to a suitable class of many-body interactions. Our results for spin systems with continuous symmetry (Sect. 3) are presented, again for simplicity, only for the case that the spins take values in the sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {S}}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Sn-1 in the setup of nearest-neighbor interactions which are invariant under all rotations in O(n), and when the noised observables are fixed to be the spins themselves.

Translation-invariance of the systems: While the proof of Aizenman and Wehr requires to work in a translation-invariant setup, the techniques developed in this article apply to spin systems which do not satisfy this assumption.

Uniformity of the results in the temperature and external field: The results of Aizenman–Wehr apply not only for a fixed value of the temperature and external magnetic field (the latter is incorporated into the models there) but also uniformly when these parameters are themselves allowed to depend on the disorder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. This uniformity shows that there cannot be deviations from the proven behavior at random critical points. In comparison, our results are stated only for a fixed value of the temperature and, in the general two-dimensional setup, without an external magnetic field (though one can be included in the base Hamiltonian). Still, a uniform version of our results may be obtained with minor modifications of the proof, as indicated in Remark 7.6.

The noised observables are taken to be the spins themselves and the conclusion is then that in all dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4, a suitable spatially and thermally averaged magnetization value is invariant under all elements of G for all Gibbs measures of the model, almost surely. It is further mentioned that the technique should generalize to a suitable class of many-body interactions. Our results for spin systems with continuous symmetry (Sect. 3) are presented, again for simplicity, only for the case that the spins take values in the sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {S}}^{n-1}$$\end{document}Sn-1 in the setup of nearest-neighbor interactions which are invariant under all rotations in O(n), and when the noised observables are fixed to be the spins themselves.

The noised observables are taken to be the spins themselves and the conclusion is then that in all dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\le 4$$\end{document}d≤4, a suitable spatially and thermally averaged magnetization value is invariant under all elements of G for all Gibbs measures of the model, almost surely.
